# Manuscript movement review

Project root: /Users/drive/fractured-path-monroe13
This is a compiled prompt, not evidence that a model ran or chapters were written.

---

## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

---

## REVIEW COVERAGE

8 files supplied in declared order. Only claim this coverage. If the movement is incomplete, say so. Editorial canon evidence is present only when supplied below. Without it, report apparent inconsistency, not verified canon violations.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/manuscript/chapter-54.md

# Chapter 54 — First Bell

The ring by the pump had been swept, and Cael stood in the middle of the bare place where it had been, in the dark before the first bell, and did the oldest footwork he knew.

It was not practice. Lira had a word for it, and the word was upkeep: the thing you did to a blade you were not going to use that day, so that it would still be a blade on the day you needed it. He went through the first-year patterns from the yard behind the grain exchange, slowly, with his breath going up white and the frozen earth ridged under his boots where the broom had dragged it, and he let his feet find the ridges and stop minding them. Nobody was watching. The stable windows were dark and the residence wing was dark, and the only light on the whole hill was a lamp in the kitchen where somebody was already raking out the ovens. He did the patterns once at walking speed and once at the speed of a bout, and on the second time through the pivot came on time, the same as it had come at dusk the evening before, and he stopped there, because Lira had told him a hundred times that the last one should be the good one.

He went in and dressed by the window without lighting the lamp.

His coat hung on the back of the chair, where it had hung all night. He put it on and buttoned it, and as he did up the second button his hand went flat against the inside pocket without his telling it to, and stayed there for a moment. Two folded papers lay against his chest, one a little stiffer than the other. He did not take them out. He knew what they said, and he knew that if he read them now he would be reading them for luck, which was not what either of them had been given to him for.

He went down to breakfast with his hands empty.

Karis was already at the end of the scarred table, and she was talking to herself.

She did it very quietly, with her eyes on her bowl and her spoon not moving, and he knew what she was saying before he could hear the words, because he had heard them every night for a week in a flat voice that was not hers. *The parties are named in the notice.* Her lips moved on the next sentence, and the next, and her free hand lay on the table beside her bowl with a small stiff card under it, face down.

Lira sat down on her other side, looked at the card, and took it.

She did not ask. She slid it out from under Karis's fingers the way she would have taken a heel of bread off a market stall, and put it in the inside pocket of her own coat, and buttoned the pocket, and went on eating.

"I needed that," said Karis.

"You don't need it. You know it. You've known it since Friday." Lira tore her bread in half. "If you say it once more he'll hear it in your voice instead of the magistrate's, and then he'll be listening for you all morning, and you won't be there to say it." The larger half went to Cael without her looking. "It's in my pocket with his pencil and his chalk. You can have all three back at supper."

Karis looked at the pocket for a moment as if she were weighing whether to take it back by force. Then she picked up her spoon.

"That's the second time you've confiscated a document from me," she said.

"I'll keep count for you," said Lira.

Brom said nothing at all. He sat across from Cael and ate his barley steadily and slowly, as he ate it every morning, and when he had finished he set his spoon down in the bowl and folded his big hands on the wood. Cael waited for whatever he was going to say. Brom looked at him for a while, and then he looked at the window, and then he looked back.

"That's my whole contribution," said Brom. "I thought about it most of the night. I decided the most useful thing I could do this morning was not say anything clever."

"You've never said anything clever in your life," said Lira.

"That's why I'm so good at it." Brom picked up his cup. "Eat. You'll be in that room till the light goes, and I'm told they don't feed the respondent."

Cael ate. He had thought he would not be able to, and found that his body had decided otherwise without consulting him, the way it had decided on every morning before every floor since he was twelve. Round them the stable was louder than it had ever been at this hour. The second-years were up and dressed in their good coats, and the tout had a crowd round him by the fire, and twice somebody came past the end of the table and slowed and said something to Brom about the gallery. Nobody spoke to Cael, which he understood was a kindness.

When the first bell rang, all four of them stood up at once without anybody having arranged it.

---

They walked him across the yard and into the lecture wing and along the ground-floor passage to the assembly room, the four of them together, and the passage was so full that people pressed back against the walls to let them through.

At the rope they stopped. There was a cord strung across the room on iron posts, waist high, with the benches behind it and the open floor in front, and a clerk at the gap in it with a list. Brom went first. He put one hand on Cael's shoulder in passing, briefly and very heavily, and went in past the clerk to the gallery without turning round. Karis went next. She did not touch him; she looked at him once, levelly, the look she gave a timing that had come out where she had predicted, and went in after Brom. Lira went last. Her hand came down on his shoulder and stayed there, and her fingers closed, hard, and she did not say anything, because she had said all of it in a swept yard at dusk the day before and she was not a person who said a thing twice. Then she let go and was gone into the benches, and he was standing at the gap in the rope by himself.

The clerk looked at her list, and then at him, and lifted the cord.

He walked out onto the floor.

It was perhaps twenty paces from the rope to the respondent's table across scrubbed boards, and he had decided on the stair, without quite putting it into words, that he would walk them the way he walked out to the chalk before a bout he expected to lose. He did not hurry, and he did not look at his feet. Two hundred people were watching him do it, and more in the passage behind them, and the room could make of him whatever it liked; it would anyway. The one thing he did not mean to give it was a boy who looked as though he shared its worst opinion of him. He walked the twenty paces at an even pace with his hands at his sides and his shoulders where Lira would have wanted them, and he counted the room while he did it, because counting a room had always been the steadiest thing in him.

The assembly room had never held so many. Benches from the training hall had been carried in and set end to end behind the rope, and people sat on them hip to hip, and more stood along the back wall under the high windows, and the double doors at the far end had been propped wide open on the passage, where they stood three deep. He saw faces he knew in rows: second-years in their good coats, the tout with his hands for once empty, the heat-Paths instructor with his scorched cuffs, the taxonomy master with his pointer held across his knees. Wray was standing against the side wall with her arms folded. Gerda sat two rows from the front in her patched coat, very upright. On the front bench at the left, by the aisle, Oona sat with her slate on her knees and her new tag polished, and on the bench behind her, with her arms folded and her face giving away nothing, was Lira.

And among them, scattered, sitting very still, were the people who did not belong to the hill.

He counted seven. Two wore the plain grey of registry offices, and sat apart from each other as if they had arrived separately and did not mean to be seen together. One was a woman in a dark blue coat with an academy's badge at the collar that he did not know, and she had taken a seat at the end of a bench where she had a clear line not to the panel but to the recording desk, which told him she cared more about what would be written than about what would be said. There was a man with a guild's legal satchel on his knees, who had already uncapped his pen. There was an old woman in black who had brought a cushion. And there were two more he could not place at all, who looked at nothing and wrote nothing, and whose stillness was the most deliberate thing in the room.

Seven people had come up a hill in midwinter to watch a hearing at an academy most of them could not have found on a map a month ago. The record was public, and word of it had travelled faster than three weeks ought to have let it. He took that in, and set it down where he kept the things he would think about later, and reached the table.

---

The room had been made over with the care of a place that knew it was going to be written about.

The lecturer's dais at the end had been raised on the platform from the assembly stage and turned into a bench, with three high-backed chairs behind it and a long green cloth over it, and above it the roof beams of the old waystation ran across the whole width of the hall, black with age, so close together that the plaster between them looked like light coming through a fence. Below the bench, on its own small platform, sat the recording desk. There were two tables on the open floor. The delegation's stood on the left, squared with document cases, and the respondent's stood on the right, bare except for a slate and a stick of chalk that were not his. A little behind and to one side was a third, where the academy's counsel sat with a satchel of papers and Naveth beside him, very straight, with his long hands folded on the wood.

Quenna was already at the respondent's table. She had the slate in front of her and the chalk beside it and nothing else at all, and when he sat down she did not look at him. She looked at the bench.

"Empty hands," she said, very quietly. "Good."

"Lira took my pencil."

"Lira has more sense than half the counsel I've ever sat beside." Her eyes did not move. "Listen to all of it. Don't answer any of it. Not on your face, not with your hands. Today is his."

Across the floor Coss had come in through the side door with his aide behind him, and he was laying out his cases on the delegation's table in an order that had plainly been decided long before he reached the room. He did not look at the respondent's table. He looked at the cases, and then at the bench, and then he sat down with his hands flat on the closest case and waited, as still as a man at a desk in a borrowed office in Ardenmere, with the patience that had always made him so hard to read.

Behind Cael, toward the back wall, there was a small sound of somebody refusing to give up a seat, and a dry voice he knew informing somebody else that the bench was not for climbing over. He did not turn round. He knew where Prynn was without turning, the way he knew where his own hands were.

The recording officer came in by the door below the bench, carrying the sloped wooden case, and set it down on the desk and opened it, and wound the roll a little on its spindles and stood the brass glass upright at the top corner. He did not look up at the room. He uncapped his ink and laid out two pens side by side, and then a third, and Cael watched him do it and thought that whatever else happened today, that young man was going to write down every word of it.

Then a door at the back of the dais opened, and the room stood up.

The panel came in one after another. Yorlan came first, long and narrow and grey, with a single thin volume under his arm, and he took the middle chair without looking at the room he had made stand. Ilsev came after him and took the chair on his right, a small upright woman in a dark coat, with nothing in her hands at all. The third was a stranger. He was a broad grey-bearded man in assessor's grey who had come up the hill at dawn, the clerk had told Quenna, by rota from the next district, and who had so far said nothing to anybody that was not about where to put his coat. He took the left-hand chair and set a pad of paper in front of him and squared it.

On the corner of the bench, at Ilsev's elbow, somebody had set a stack of dark-spined volumes, closed. Cael knew them without being able to read their spines from where he stood. He had held every one of them.

Yorlan opened the thin book, and the room sat.

---

He said it without looking up, in a voice that gave every word exactly the weight of the word before it, as if the sentences had long ago been worn so smooth that nothing in them could catch.

"This tribunal sits under the charter, in public, and on the record. The matter is named in the notice. The parties are named in the notice. The panel will hear the challenge first, and the answer after. The panel will hear what is put to it and no more. The panel will not hear argument for which it has not been shown the ground. The respondent will state his name."

It was word for word what Karis had said behind a wall of books in the stable on the Friday night, in the same order and at the same pace, and something in Cael's chest turned over at the exactness of it. It was not fear. It was nearer the feeling of stepping onto a floor he had walked in the dark a hundred times and finding every board where he had left it.

He stood.

"Caelen Hesk-ward."

"The panel notes it." Yorlan turned a page. "The respondent may sit."

He sat. Yorlan read the notice into the record, the matter and the parties, the challenge filed by the Compact's field service against the enrollment of the candidate on the unclassified observer track, the academy's counsel for the academy, the respondent for himself. He read the challenge itself in the same voice, every clause of it as level as a list of goods on a carter's bill, so that the sentences which had taken the whole of Cael's winter away from him went past in the room no louder than the scrape of a bench. At the recording desk the young man's pen moved without stopping. Then Yorlan closed the thin book on his finger and looked, for the first time, at the room in front of him, not at any one person in it but at all of it.

"The panel will hear the challenge," he said. "The officer of record."

Coss stood up.

He did not look at the respondent's table, and Cael found that he had expected that and was glad of it. It was not coldness. There was nothing personal on that side of the floor, and there never had been, and the whole of what Coss meant to show the room today rested on that.

"Magistrate. Assessors." Coss spoke as he always spoke, as if he were reading a line off a sheet, though there was no sheet in his hand. "The Compact will put three things before the panel. First, what the respondent's provision was written to do, and for whom: its text says so, and so do its drafting records, and the Compact will produce both. Second, that the respondent is not one of the people it was written for: that is in the registry, and in his own words on deposition. Third, that whenever a chartered academy has stretched this provision over a person it was not written for, the enrollment has been voided, every time, and the Compact will produce each instance." He paused, not for effect, only because the next sentence was a separate thing and he gave it a separate place. "What the respondent is, the Compact leaves alone. That question is not before you, and the Compact will not put it there. The Compact asks the panel to read a paper and say what it says. The paper is plain. The Compact begins with its text."

He sat down. It had taken less time than it takes to walk across a yard.

Somewhere in the gallery behind Cael somebody let out a breath, and somebody else hushed them, and the clerk at the rope looked round, and the room went quiet again. Cael understood what the room had just been told, though most of it would not have been able to say. A man who did not need to argue had told a panel that he did not need to argue, and had then sat down so as not to spoil it.

Coss read the provision first.

He read it in full, from the certified copy with Prynn's pencil at its foot, every sentence of it from *a candidate without a standard classification issued by an Arbiter* to *the panel's finding shall be entered in the candidate's record*. He read it slowly, and when he reached the end Yorlan said, "Again, for the roll," and he read it again at exactly the same pace, with the same small pauses in the same places, so that the second reading sounded like the first laid over itself. Then he took the enrollment form, the certified copy of the one Cael had signed on his first afternoon, and laid it on the delegation's table beside the provision, and read the category line from it. *Unclassified observer.* Then he read the gloss beneath it. *Has not yet undergone formal Kindling assessment.* He read that twice as well, without being asked.

Then he let it sit.

He did not say anything about it. He stood beside the two papers with his hands at his sides and let the room look at them, and the room looked, and Cael, at the respondent's table with his hands flat in his lap, watched him do it and felt something he had not expected to feel on this morning of all mornings, which was admiration.

He knew how to watch a fighter. He had been doing it since before he could have said what he was doing, from the back of crowds in Ardenmere, from the rail of the training hall, from a bench in the formal yard; and he found, sitting here, that he could not stop doing it now, even with his own place on the hill as the purse. Coss fought low. He did not reach. He set one thing down and stood on it before he set down the next, so that by the time anybody saw where he was going he was already there, with his weight over his feet and nothing behind him that could be knocked away. He put nothing on the floor that Cael could pick up tomorrow and turn round on him. Every piece he laid was a piece that was true, and that would still be true whatever anybody said afterward, and he laid them in the order a mason lays a course of stone, one at a time, each one bearing on the one beneath.

It was the deposition again, Cael thought. *Answer the question asked.* Only now it was the other man doing it, for a whole day, in front of everybody, and doing it better than anybody Cael had ever watched.

He would have liked to write that down. He had nothing to write it with. Under the table, against his knee, he folded down one finger of his left hand, the way Brom made a mark in his number column, and kept it folded. *Text. Theirs.*

---

The drafting records came next, and they changed the room behind him.

Coss took them out of the second case one at a time, each in a folder of faded card with a slip on it, and entered each by its number. Cael had known they existed since the morning in Naveth's office, and he had walked the whole road of the provision's history himself in eleven hours and found it went nowhere, and he had known, at the end of that road, that Coss would be standing there with the first draft in his case. He had not known what else would be in it.

The first was a memorandum of the committee that had written the provision, a long time ago, stating what it was for: academic access for the untested children of member families, travelling on a guild's licence, until such time as they could be brought to a station and assessed. Coss read it plainly, and laid it down. The second was the first draft itself, the one Cael had read in the archive in its leaning, tired-looking hand, and Coss read the first two sentences of it and laid that down too.

The third was a draft Cael had never seen.

It was a later page, from the same committee in the same years, proposing that the provision should also cover *candidates whose capabilities present irregularly and cannot be assessed under the standard schedule*. Below the proposal, in a different ink, somebody had recorded the committee's vote. The proposal had been struck. And below the vote, in a third hand, somebody had written down the reason, in two plain sentences: that the provision was a kindness to children awaiting assessment, and was not to be made a door for anybody the schedule could not hold; and that if such persons were ever to be provided for, it should be by the schedule and not by this.

There were signatures under it. Coss read them out, one by one, as names, without comment.

Behind Cael, at the academy's table, something changed in Naveth.

Cael did not turn round. He did not need to. He had sat across a desk from the provost often enough in twenty weeks to know the man's stillnesses, the way he knew Hobb's or Brom's, and this was a new one: not the careful blank he kept for a records request, but the stillness of somebody who has just been shown a door he had walked through for forty years and learned, in front of two hundred people, that somebody had once put a sign on the far side of it, and that nobody had told him.

Nobody had ever told the provost that the Compact still had those papers. Nobody at Greyvane had known. Counsel had not found them, and Cael had not found them, and Prynn, who knew where every page on the hill was kept, had never had them to find, because they were not on the hill. They had been in a basement room under a regional chamber, in a box marked for something else, waiting for one man with the patience to look.

"Nobody forgot the respondent when this provision was written," said Coss, setting the last folder square on the others. "It leaves him out on purpose. The committee was asked, in its own day, whether it ought to reach a person like him. It said no. Its no is signed."

Cael folded down a second finger under the table.

He sat with the two folded fingers against his knee and thought about what he had just learned, coldly, because it was worth learning. The Compact kept everything. Whatever it had ever written down, about anything, it still had somewhere, in a box in a basement, under the wrong name perhaps, but kept. An argument that counted on some old page having gone missing had nothing under its feet.

And his own argument hoped for exactly the opposite. It hoped that every page the Compact had ever written was still exactly where it had been put, and that he could stand up tomorrow and ask the room to read all of them, and that none of them would say what everybody in that room had always taken it for granted they said.

He looked at the dark-spined volumes at Ilsev's elbow, and then away from them.

Beside him, without turning her head, Quenna drew the slate a hand's width toward herself and wrote on it in small square letters, and then turned it a quarter toward him so that he could read it, and then turned it back and wiped it with the side of her hand before anybody else could.

*Let him have every one of these. You never wanted them.*

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/manuscript/chapter-55.md

# Chapter 55 — Not Allowed to Hit Anybody

Lira had been told three times that she was not allowed to hit anybody, and by the second hour she had begun to keep a list of the people she was not allowed to hit.

Quenna had told her first, on the stair the week before, in the voice she used for the record. Gerda had told her second, at supper, with the exact words of the procedure: a member of the gallery who disturbed the sitting might be removed by the clerk, and a cough counted, if the clerk decided it counted. Cael had told her third, at dusk in the swept yard, by asking her to write it down, which was worse than being told. So she sat on the second bench from the front at the left, by the aisle, directly behind Oona's straight back and polished tag, with her arms folded so that her hands would have somewhere to be, and she kept the list in her head instead.

The tout was on it. He was four rows back and to the right, with his crust book open on his knee, and an hour into the sitting he had started taking wagers again in a voice he thought was low. She had heard the odds. Two second-years by the windows were on it, because every time the Warden laid another paper down one of them breathed *that's it, then* to the other, as if they had been asked. The man from the guild's legal office was on it for the way he smiled at his own pen. And the Warden was at the top of it, not because he had done anything she could have named, but because he stood at that table in his road-coloured coat and laid papers down one after another in his patient voice, and every paper was true, and she could feel the room coming to believe each one as it landed, and there was nothing whatever to hit.

She had watched a great many fights from a great many benches. She knew the sound a crowd made when the fighter it had come to cheer started losing slowly, and the room was making it now. It was not a noise so much as a change in the quiet. At the first bell the benches had been bright and tight with the feeling of people come to see their own boy win something; she had heard it in the passage, in the second-years' good coats, in the tout's first odds. Now, two papers in, the brightness had gone flat and uneasy, and people had begun to shift on the benches and look at their hands.

Cael had not moved at all. He sat at the bare table with his hands in his lap and his eyes on the Warden, and from where she sat she could see the back of his head and one ear and the set of his shoulders, which were exactly where she had put them the evening before. She watched them the way she would have watched a fighter's hips. They did not come up once.

*Good,* she thought. *Stay there.*

---

At the second hour the magistrate said, "The panel will hear the witnesses of fact entered for the respondent, at the hour named," and the academy's counsel stood up.

He was a narrow grey man with a satchel, engaged from the regional town in the autumn, and Lira had never heard him say anything before. He said very little now. He named Edran, and Edran came out of the front of the gallery through the gap in the rope and walked to the place on the open floor where witnesses stood, a square of boards marked at its corners with chalk, and stood in it with his arms at his sides.

She had not liked Edran for most of a term. She had changed her mind about that slowly and against her will, starting on the morning he stood in the formal yard and withdrew his petition in his own hand, and she had not told anybody she had changed it, least of all him. She watched him now with the old dislike and the new respect sitting side by side in her, neither one giving way.

Counsel asked him to say what he had seen.

Edran said it. He said it as he had said he would, plainly, in the flat honest voice that had always made him sound a little older than he was. He had watched every one of the candidate's sittings that anybody was let in to see. He had fought him once, in the exhibition, in the formal yard, under standings rules, and lost. He described the loss without softening it: the gap in his own sequence where his third structure came up cold, and the way the candidate had found it on the second exchange and gone into it, and found it again. He had watched the match against Karis from the bench by the wall. He had watched the sixth sitting, and had seen three marks put on a post at a size named in advance. Where he was sure, he said so; where he was not, he said that too, and the not-being-sure made the rest weigh more.

"Thank you," said counsel, and sat down.

Lira let out a breath she had not known she was keeping.

"The officer of record may put questions," said the magistrate.

The Warden stood. He did not come round his table. He stood behind it with his hands at his sides and looked at Edran across the open boards, and Lira thought he looked at him as if he had already read everything Edran was going to say and was only checking the copy.

"You were first on the standings of this academy," said the Warden. "Until this term."

"Until this term," said Edran. Lira saw what it cost him, and saw him pay it without wincing, and so did everybody in the gallery who knew him.

"This term you put your name to a petition against the respondent's place on the training floor. And withdrew it."

"Yes."

"One question, then." The Warden's voice did not change at all. "This respondent was enrolled under a provision written for children who haven't yet been to a station. In your own judgment, as a practitioner who has fought him, at what level does he fight?"

Edran did not look at the respondent's table. He looked at the Warden.

"Iron-tier, at minimum," he said. "Perhaps higher. I couldn't say how much."

It went through the gallery like cold water.

Lira felt it go. She felt the two second-years by the window lean together, and heard the tout's pencil, and she watched the back of Cael's head and saw it not move. The Warden did not underline the answer. He did not turn to the panel or say *thank you* in the way men say it when they have been handed exactly what they came for. He only said, "Thank you. Nothing further," and sat down, and left the answer standing in the middle of the floor by itself, where it looked much larger than if he had pointed at it.

Edran did not leave the chalk square at once.

"I don't know what it was," he said, to the bench, before the clerk could lift the rope for him. "What he did. I'd like that on the record as well. I'm not saying it to help him. It's only true."

"The panel notes it," said the magistrate, and Edran went back to his bench with his flat honest face giving nothing away, and sat down, and folded his arms.

Lira found, to her own annoyance, that she wanted to stand up and shake his hand. She sat on her own hands instead.

Hobb took less time.

Counsel named him, and he came through the rope with his feet a shoulder-width apart, as he walked everywhere, and stood in the chalk square as if he had been asked to hold up one end of the room. Counsel asked him how many of the candidate's sittings he had stood in as the partner. Hobb thought about it for so long that somebody in the back of the gallery coughed, and the clerk looked round.

"Four," said Hobb.

Counsel asked him whether anything had been different in any of them. Hobb thought about that too.

"Same as last time," he said. "Four times."

There was a sound from the gallery that was very nearly a laugh, and the clerk turned round properly this time, and it stopped.

"The officer of record may put questions," said the magistrate.

The Warden looked at Hobb for a moment across the boards, and Lira, watching, could have sworn that something at the corner of his mouth moved and then thought better of it.

"In your judgment," said the Warden, "what Path does the respondent practise?"

"Didn't say," said Hobb. "Said what I saw."

"Nothing further," said the Warden.

Hobb went back through the rope and sat down beside Edran, and Edran moved along the bench a hand's width to give him room without looking at him, and that was all. In front of Lira, Oona bent over her slate and wrote something and underlined it twice, and Lira did not need to see it. She would have bet her bread it said *two b's*, or *eight words*, or both.

It was the best testimony anybody would give all day, Lira thought, and nobody in the room but Brom would know it.

---

Before the midday bell the Warden came back to the form.

He did it so quietly that half the gallery did not notice the case had changed direction. He took the certified copy of the enrollment form from the first case, where it had lain since the morning, and set it on the green cloth's edge in front of the bench. Beside it he laid a single registry sheet, which Lira could not read from where she sat but could guess at. Then he read the gloss under the category line once more, slowly. *Has not yet undergone formal Kindling assessment.* Then he read from the registry sheet the name of a station in Denvash, and a date, and the word *assessed*.

"The form says one thing," said the Warden, "and the registry says the other, and the registry was there first. The panel need not take the Compact's word for which is so. It has both in front of it." He squared the two sheets together. "The enrollment is invalid on its own face. The Compact will show the panel that the respondent knew it."

Lira saw Oona's pencil stop.

Then he read the deposition into the record.

He had kept it for the end, after the provision and the drafting records, the witnesses and the form, and Lira understood, sitting on her hands, that he had saved it, the way a fighter saves the strike he has been setting up for three exchanges. He did not stand any differently or speak any louder. He took a bound fair copy from the third case, in the young recorder's best hand, and opened it at a marked page, and began.

He read the respondent's name and number as the registry held them. He read the question about the enrollment form and the signature, and the answers, which were *yes*, and the date. He read the question about how many times the deponent had read the category line before he signed it, and the answer. "*Three.*" Somewhere behind Lira a girl drew in her breath. He read the questions about the circuit at Ardenmere, and the answer that the circuit's ledger said so. He read the question about what the deponent's architecture was, in his own words, and the answer, "*That's a classification question,*" and the question after it, and the answer, "*I understand that everything may be noted. That's what a record is.*" At that one a ripple went along the front bench that was not quite anything, and Lira could not tell whether it was approval or the start of a laugh, and neither, she thought, could the people making it.

He read the question about the sixth sitting. *Before that sitting, was that capability entered in any record of this academy?* "*No.*" *Why not?* "*Because I hadn't elected to show it. The provision lets the candidate elect.*" *Had you possessed it for some time?* "*Four weeks.*"

He read that without any weight on it, and went on to the next page, and Lira saw the panel's three heads all go down at once to their papers and come up again.

Then he read the question.

"*When you signed the enrollment form, did you understand the category to describe you accurately?*" The Warden paused. "*No.*"

He looked down at the page, and then up, not at the gallery and not at the respondent but at the bench.

"The record marks a silence of nine counts after the answer," he said.

At the recording desk below the bench the young recorder's pen stopped moving. Lira saw it stop. She saw him look down at his own roll, and then not look at anything.

"*Then why did you sign it?*" read the Warden. "*Because it was the door the provision had, and I wanted to go to school.*"

He closed the fair copy.

Lira had heard all of it before. She had heard it at the scarred table on the Wednesday, in Cael's own voice, with his bread in front of him and his hands turning it over; she had torn that bread in half and given him both halves because she had not known what else to do with her hands. She had heard it then as a true thing told among friends. She was hearing it now in a stranger's patient voice, read off a stranger's page to two hundred people and three officers of the Compact and seven people who had come up a hill to listen, and it was the same words and not the same thing at all.

The gallery made no sound for a moment. Then it made a low, confused one, and the confusion was the part Lira had not expected. She had expected contempt. She had braced for the noise a crowd makes when it hears the fighter it cheered for admit he took a dive. That was not this. Somebody near the windows had said *oh*, softly, the way you say it when you see a small child fall, and somebody else had said *he wanted to go to school* in a voice that did not know whether it was sorry for him or for itself. The tout had stopped writing. In front of her, Oona's back had gone very straight and very still, and her pencil was not moving on the slate at all, which Lira had never once seen.

And the Warden stood beside his cases and let it lie, as he had let everything lie, and Lira understood for the first time why Cael had stopped being angry at him. The man had not twisted it. He had not needed to. He had read the boy's own words exactly as they were said, the nine counts included, and the words were true, and they were the best stone in his wall, and he had put them in place without once pretending they were anything else.

She was still not allowed to hit him. She found that she no longer particularly wanted to, and she found that worse.

"The panel will rise for the midday interval," said the magistrate. "The sitting resumes at the fifth bell."

---

Brom was the one who found the corner.

The yard had filled the moment the doors opened, all of it in knots, all of it talking low, and the four of them came out into it and found that every knot turned to look. Brom did not say anything. He walked them across the yard as if he were carrying something heavy that might slip, round the end of the lecture wing, to the narrow angle where its end wall met the old wall of the yard, which was out of the wind and out of the way and had nothing in it but a broken barrow. He stood in the mouth of the angle with his back to the yard and folded his arms, and the yard, after a moment, found something else to look at.

Lira had the heel of a loaf in her coat, because she always did. She broke it and gave Cael the larger part. He held it and did not eat it.

Karis did not wait for anybody to begin. She had her notebook shut under her arm, which in Karis meant that everything she needed was already written down and she was only thinking now.

"There's one thing about his case I want you to have," she said, "before you go back in." She looked at Cael and at nobody else. "It's whole. I don't mean strong; strong is a different word. I mean there's nothing missing from it and nothing held back. He told the panel three things at the first bell. He's proved two and the third is lying in his last case waiting for after the interval, and not one of them needs anything from you to stand up. He hasn't kept a single paper for tomorrow." She tapped the notebook against her palm, once. "Nothing's being kept back, because in his head tomorrow is only the day the panel reads out its ruling. So the moment you stand up, he's got nothing ready. Anything he says after that, he'll have to invent standing up. He hasn't had to do that once in three weeks."

"Is that good?" said Lira.

"It's the only thing I've seen since the first bell that I'd call good," said Karis. "So I'm going to hold on to it."

Brom had not turned round. He was looking out of the mouth of the angle at the far door of the assembly room, where the knots of people stood and talked and did not go in.

"He fights like you," said Brom.

Lira looked at the back of his head. "Meaning what?"

"Meaning he waits." Brom still did not turn. "He doesn't give you anything early. He sets everything down where it's safe, one thing at a time, and he doesn't put his weight on any of it until he knows what you've got. Then he puts it all in at once, at the end." He turned his head at last and looked at Cael. "That's how you fight. You always have. I've stood across from it. I've never had to sit and watch it from the outside before, done at somebody I'm on the side of. It's very hard to sit still for."

Cael was looking at the same door. He had not eaten any of the bread.

"Someone who commits later," he said.

Brom nodded, once, as if a lift had been called right, and turned back to the yard.

Lira waited until Karis had gone to the pump for a cup of water and Brom had gone with her, because there was a question she did not want to ask in front of either of them.

"Is it bad?" she said. "In there. I can't tell. I've watched a hundred fights and I can't tell. Everybody on my bench thinks it's finished."

"It's what we said it would be." He turned the bread over. "That isn't bad. That's the plan, being done to us, the way we said it would be."

"I know it's the plan. I sat at the table every night while you made it." She heard her voice climbing and brought it down. "I didn't know that sitting on a bench while the other man carries out his half would feel like this. Nobody told me. I'd like to have been told."

"I didn't know either," said Cael. He looked at the bread in his hand and finally ate some of it, slowly. "He hasn't laid one stone on me. Every one of them's on the enrollment, and I'm going to give him the enrollment before I say anything else. All day he's been building me a floor." He swallowed. "Even the last stone. The *no*. That was always going to be his. All I did was hand it over on the Wednesday instead of the Monday."

She watched him finish the bread, all of it. She watched him wipe his fingers on his coat and lay his hand flat over the inside pocket for a moment, where she knew her slip lay against Brom's page. When the fifth bell went across the yard he straightened off the wall, and she checked his shoulders out of habit, and they were exactly where they belonged. He walked back across the yard at an even pace with his hands empty, and the knots of people at the door moved apart to let him through.

She stood in the angle of the walls and watched him go in. Her hands had made fists in her coat pockets without asking her, and she let them stay that way all the way back to her bench.

---

The afternoon began with the dead.

Cael thought of it that way afterward, though the Warden would never have put it like that and nobody in the room would have known what he meant. Coss took a fourth case from beside his table, and from it three folders tied with tape, and laid them side by side on the green cloth's edge where the bench could reach them, and entered them as the precedents of the Compact.

He gave each its district and its year and its name. The first was Sarnholt. A chartered academy there had enrolled under the provision a youth who was past the age of Kindling and had refused to be tested, on the reasoning that a person who would not be assessed had not yet been assessed. The enrollment had been voided inside a single sitting. The second was Wexley, where a registry clerk had made an honest error that nobody caught for three years, so that a girl who had Kindled and been classified was entered on an academy's rolls as untested. When it came to light the academy had defended the enrollment on the ground of the girl's good standing, and lost, and had spent two years under review of its own charter for having tried. The third had no name read out, only its district and its year, because it had never come to a hearing at all. A disputed assessment, an enrollment under the provision, a challenge filed; and the academy had withdrawn the enrollment before the magistrate arrived.

"In each instance the enrollment was voided," said Coss. "In none was the provision found to reach a person it was not written for. The Compact is not aware of any instance in which it has been so found." He put his hand flat on the third folder. "In every one of them, the institution either withdrew, or argued, and lost. There has never been a sound argument for this enrollment to make. The provision says what it says. It always has."

Under the table Cael folded down a third finger. *Precedent. Theirs. Unbroken.*

He was right, Cael thought, about every case but the one that had never been brought. And there was nothing on any page in the room to tell anybody that, yet.

Then Coss came to the records of the sittings, and entered them as capability.

He read Wray's line from the first, the seven words she had chosen and counted and would stand by, and at the side wall Cael saw her not move. He read the second's and the third's. He read Quenna's from the fifth, the match that had been halted, word for word, in the voice he used for everything. "*Atypical adaptive response, consistent with previously documented irregular architecture. No safety violation noted. Assessment satisfied under demonstration provision.*" And then he took the last sheet in the folder, which was the sixth, and held it a moment before he read it, as if to be sure of the date.

"*The candidate demonstrated, at his own election, an ignition-point capability, contact-adjacent, single channel. Three times, at a size stated in advance, with a rest between. Consistent, observable practice. No safety violation noted. Assessment satisfied.*" He laid it down. "Entered as of its own date. The Compact offers the record of the sittings as the academy's own account of what the respondent can do. The Compact does not dispute a word of it."

Cael sat with his three fingers folded against his knee and heard the three small black rings on the grey oak go past him in the room, in the dullest language Quenna owned. Nobody laughed. Nobody gasped. A man in a grey coat near the back wrote something down. In the front of the gallery he knew without looking that a girl with a polished tag was holding her pencil very still over a slate on which she had once written that she had seen the first one.

It held. That was what he noticed most, sitting there. The dullest record in the room had been read aloud by the man who most wanted to make something of it, and there had been nothing in it to make anything of. It was a thing with a size. It did what it was said to do, three times, with a rest between. It held.

He folded down a fourth finger. *Records. Theirs. All true.*

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/manuscript/chapter-56.md

# Chapter 56 — The Answer After

"The challenge rests on documents," said Yorlan. "The panel will hear the officers of the respondent institution who made them, as to the documents and no further. The presiding assessor of the candidate's sittings."

Quenna stood up from beside him.

She did not touch the table as she rose, or look at him, or do anything at all that a room could have read as a word passed between them. She walked to the chalk square on the open boards where Edran and Hobb had stood that morning, and stood in it with her hands folded in front of her and her slate left behind on the respondent's table, face down, beside his empty hands.

Coss stood behind his cases.

"Assessor. You presided at each of the respondent's six sittings under the provision."

"I did."

"And wrote, in each of them, the lines the panel has just heard."

"I drafted my own lines. The instructor's seat and the rotating seat drafted theirs."

"Your own lines, then." Coss did not raise his voice, and he did not lower it, and he did not look at anything but her. "You hold the Silver tier, and you have sat as an assessor in this district for twenty years. You chose these words." He laid two fingers on the sixth sitting's sheet. "You chose every one of them."

"I chose every one of them."

"Out of how many you might have used?"

For a moment Quenna said nothing. Cael, at the bare table with his hands in his lap, watched her decide to answer the question that had been asked and not the one beneath it.

"A great many," she said.

"Then I'll ask the one under it plainly, Assessor, so that neither of us has to pretend I haven't." Coss's hands were still. "When you wrote these lines, were you choosing words to say what you saw? Or were you choosing words that would say as little of what you saw as could be said and still be true?"

The room went so quiet that Cael heard the sand in the brass glass on the recording desk.

He had known this question was coming. He had known it since the morning in Naveth's office, when she had said that the next three weeks could have whatever they wanted of her; he had known it every time he had read one of her records, which he had done until he could have recited them. And knowing did not help at all, because the person standing in the chalk square was the person who had put her name to him in front of a panel when nobody else on the hill would, and she was about to be asked, in front of everybody, to choose between that and the truth.

She did not take long.

"Every record is a choice," said Quenna. "No assessor ever wrote down everything she saw. She couldn't. She'd be writing still." Her voice was the voice of the record, flat and dry, and it did not tremble anywhere. "I chose narrowly. I chose inside the standards of my seat. I chose in a case where one loose word could do harm out of all proportion to the truth in it, and I knew that, and I wrote with it in mind." She looked at Coss, and only at Coss. "Nothing in those records is false."

Coss let it rest a moment. Then he turned his head, not to her but to the bench.

"Nothing in those records is false," he said to the panel, gently, as a man repeats a figure so that the clerk can be sure of it. He did not add anything to it. He did not have to. The sentence had been true when she said it, and it was true when he said it, and in his mouth it meant something it had not meant in hers. "Thank you, Assessor. Nothing further."

She came back to the table and sat down beside him, and took up her slate, and turned it face up, and did not look at him.

Cael did not look at her either. He found that he could not have, for a while, without something happening to his face that the room was not entitled to, and he had promised her nothing on his face. He sat and watched the bench, and felt her beside him, very straight, and thought that he had owed her more than he had ever stopped to add up, and that he had only just this moment found out how much.

---

Naveth came next, and it was harder.

It was harder because Naveth had never stood on a floor in his life. He had sat behind a desk for longer than Cael had been alive, and made careful paper for people the registry had priced wrong, and he knew a great deal about how a sentence could be built so that it held, and nothing at all about how a sentence sounded when it was read back to you across open boards by a man who had built a better one. He walked to the chalk square slowly and stood in it like somebody who has been asked to stand on ice, with his long hands hanging, and Cael saw counsel at the academy's table shut his eyes for a moment and open them.

Coss was not unkind to him once. He did not need to be.

He walked the provost through the enrollment from the beginning: who had first raised the provision, and when; who had read the category line and the gloss; what the academy's council had been told, and by whom, at the meeting in the autumn when it approved the observer track for a candidate the scout of record had brought in. He asked each question plainly and accepted each answer as it came. And every careful thing Naveth had said in that council room in the autumn, every hedge he had meant as caution, came back to him across the boards in the order it had happened. Set out in that order, it drew a man who had seen the truth on the first afternoon and had signed his name under it anyway.

"The council's minute of that meeting," said Coss, and read from it. "*The provost advises that the enrollment is defensible on the face of the form, and expects it to be tested.*" He looked up. "Defensible on its face. You chose that phrase."

"I chose it because it was accurate," said Naveth. "This academy has always—"

"The academy isn't before the panel, Provost. One form is." Coss's voice was mild. "On its face, then. And underneath?"

Naveth opened his mouth, and nothing came out of it.

Cael saw it happen and could not look away. He saw the provost's right hand come up a little way from his side, as if to rest on a rail that was not there, and stop in the air. He saw his long face go very still, and then not still. He saw the muscles along the jaw work, once, twice, as a man's jaw works when he is trying to hold something down in his throat by force, and fail to hold it. Naveth took off his spectacles. He held them in one hand by the folded arms and looked at them as if he did not know whose they were. The silence went on past where a silence ought to stop, and two hundred people sat in it, and nobody coughed.

When he spoke his voice had gone somewhere Cael had never heard it go.

"I knew the category didn't fit him," said Naveth.

He stopped. He put the spectacles back on, carefully, with both hands, the way an old man does a small thing he has done ten thousand times and must suddenly think about. His eyes behind them were wet, and he did not hide it, and he did not wipe them.

"I signed it anyway."

There was a sound from the gallery, very small, from somewhere near the front. Cael did not turn to see who had made it.

Naveth looked across the open boards at the respondent's table, at Cael, for the first time since he had stood up. He looked at him for a long moment, and whatever was in his face then was not anything the provost of Greyvane had ever let anybody see at his desk, with the door open or shut. Then he looked back at the man who had asked him the question.

"I'd sign it again."

Coss did not move for a moment.

"I don't doubt it," he said at last, and there was nothing in it but the plain respect of one careful man for another who has just paid, in public, the whole price of telling the truth. "Thank you, Provost. Nothing further."

Naveth walked back to the academy's table. He walked as he always walked, upright and slow, and he sat down beside counsel and folded his long hands on the wood, and he did not take them apart again for the rest of the day. Behind Cael the gallery made a noise that was not words and settled. When Cael finally let himself glance aside, Quenna was writing on her slate, and the chalk was moving very slowly, and he saw that she was not writing anything at all, only drawing the same short line over and over in the same place.

---

The light had gone low and yellow in the high windows by the time Coss laid down his last paper.

It had been a long afternoon, and every hour of it had belonged to him. Cael sat with all four fingers of his left hand folded down against his knee and the thumb with them, and ran the count in his head the way he would have run a scorecard in the corner after a bout he had not been allowed to enter. *Text. Theirs. Drafting records. Theirs, signed. The form's face, and the deposition. Theirs, in my own voice. Precedent. Theirs, unbroken. The records of the sittings. Theirs, every word true. The witnesses. Theirs. Both of mine.*

Every exchange to the challenge, and nothing on his side of the page but a bare table.

That was the plan. He told himself so, quite calmly, and his heart went on doing something behind his ribs that did not agree. There had never been any point answering a man who had not yet put down all he had. All day he had watched Coss lay everything down and keep nothing back, and now it was all down, and the floor was full of it.

Behind him the gallery had gone very quiet in the way he had heard rooms go quiet at the Ironyard in the last exchange of a bout that was already decided, when the crowd stops shouting because there is nothing left to shout for and begins, in a low murmur, to talk about something else. He heard the murmur begin. He heard somebody in the second row say *well, that's that*, not unkindly. He heard the benches shift.

Yorlan closed the thin book on his finger.

"The challenge is heard," he said. "The answer is for the second sitting. The panel will—"

Cael stood up.

The chair scraped on the boards behind him, loud in the high quiet room, and he heard the murmur in the gallery stop dead, as a crowd stops when a fighter who is supposed to be finished gets up off the chalk. Beside him Quenna's hand moved a finger's width on the table. It was not to stop him. He knew the difference. It was the movement of somebody setting her feet.

"I won't be defending the enrollment, Magistrate."

Yorlan did not move. He looked straight at the respondent's table, which he had not done since the morning, with the book still shut on his finger, and on his left the grey-bearded assessor's pen stopped in the middle of a word.

"I'll be challenging the Compact's jurisdiction to void it."

For the space of a breath nobody did anything at all.

Cael stood in it with his hands at his sides and felt it, the whole length of it, the way he had once felt the half-second at the Ironyard before the board went up with Reydan's name on it beside his own. He saw Ilsev, on the right of the bench, look up sharply from her papers, the first time all day he had seen her eyes leave the page. She did not look at him. She looked at the stack of dark-spined volumes at her elbow, closed, where somebody had set them that morning in case anybody needed them. She looked at them for a long moment, the way a woman looks at a tool she had brought along without expecting to have to pick it up.

And across the open boards, at the delegation's table, Cael saw something cross Coss's face and go. It was small, and complicated, and gone almost before it arrived. It was not surprise. It was the look of a man who has spent three weeks watching a lamp burn late in an archive window across a dark town, and has guessed, and has made himself not ask, and has just heard the one thing he guessed walk out onto the record in a boy's voice.

"The panel notes it," said Yorlan.

He opened the thin book again, and turned a page, and read from it, as if the answer to what had just happened had been printed there all along, which perhaps it had.

"The panel will not hear argument for which it has not been shown the ground. The respondent will show the panel its ground at the second sitting, or the panel will not hear it." He closed the book. "When it sits, the code will be on the bench in front of it. The second sitting will open at the first bell. This sitting is closed."

He struck the small wooden gavel on the bench, once.

The room went up.

It broke over the benches in one wave, like the Ironyard when an outsider takes the third exchange, so loud and so sudden that Cael felt it in the boards under his feet before he heard it. People were on their feet behind the rope. Second-years were shouting at one another, asking things none of them knew, and the tout was standing on his bench with his crust book held over his head as if it might be taken from him. Somebody near the windows had begun to laugh and could not stop. The people who did not belong to the hill were already moving: the two grey coats first, fast, toward the open doors, with the walk of men who had letters to write before the evening coach; the woman in the dark blue coat writing as she went; the man with the guild's satchel pushing along a bench and being pushed back. And through all of it, dry and level and perfectly audible, came Prynn's voice from the back wall, informing somebody with sixty years of authority behind it that he would not be climbing over her bench to get to the door any quicker, and that the door would be there when he arrived.

Cael stayed where he was, with his palm on the wood. On the circuit he had learned to hear a crowd the way he heard rain on a roof, as something happening to the room that did not need him to do anything about it, and he heard this one that way.

Across the floor Coss was packing his cases.

He did it as he had done everything that day, in order, each paper back in its folder and each folder back in its case, the drafting records and the precedents and the bound fair copy of the deposition, without hurry, the way a man puts away his tools at the end of a long day's work that has gone exactly as he planned it, which until a few breaths ago it had. His aide was talking to him in a low urgent voice, and Coss did not appear to be listening. When the last case was closed and its clasp shut he stood with his hand on it a moment.

Then he looked up, across the scrubbed boards and the noise, straight at Cael, which he had not done once since the first bell.

He gave him one nod. It was small and plain and nothing was withheld from it. It was the nod Cael had given and been given across a hundred chalk lines at the end of a first exchange, when both men have taken the other's measure and know now what kind of fight it is going to be, and when.

Cael gave it back.

Quenna's hand settled on his shoulder. The roar reached him then, all of it together, and the first sitting was done.

---

The stable was worse than the assembly room.

He could not get to the scarred table for a long time. The whole of the stable seemed to want to stand near him without speaking to him, and the second-years kept coming past the end of the table and stopping and opening their mouths and going away again. The tout sent a heel of bread down the table with his compliments, and somebody from the movement floor whom Cael had never spoken to clapped him on the back so hard that his bowl jumped. In the end Lira stood up from the bench, turned round, and looked at the room. It did not quite go quiet, but it remembered all at once that it had business at the other end.

"Eat," said Lira, sitting down again. "And don't talk. Everybody in this building wants to ask you what you meant, and if you start telling one of them you'll be telling all of them till the eighth bell. Tomorrow you'll be telling the panel. Save it."

He ate. Brom ate beside him, steadily, and every so often looked up at the room over his spoon with the expression he wore at a rail when somebody was leaning on it who ought not to be, and the somebody always moved. Karis did not eat at all. She had her notebook open beside her bowl and was writing in it with her face very close to the page, and twice she stopped and looked at Cael across the table, and opened her mouth, and shut it again, and went back to writing.

"Ask," said Cael at last.

"I'm not going to ask. I know what you're going to say. I sat behind the bench of books for a week." Karis put the pen down. "I was going to tell you something, and then I decided it would only be me talking because I can't bear to sit still. So I've written it down instead, and you can read it in a month." She closed the notebook. "His face. When you stood. Did you see it?"

"I saw it."

"I'd like to know what that was," said Karis, "more than I'd like to know almost anything. And I never will. That's what I've written down."

Brom put his spoon down in his empty bowl.

"Tomorrow," he said, "everybody in that room is going to come in thinking it's a different fight from the one it was this morning. They'll have spent all night guessing at it." He looked at Cael. "You don't guess. You know what it is. That's the advantage. Don't waste it by lying awake."

"I'll try."

"Don't try," said Brom. "Sleep. It's not a thing you try."

Lira took the card out of her coat pocket, the one she had confiscated at breakfast, and laid it on the table in front of Karis, face down, and then the pencil and the chalk in front of Cael, side by side, in a row.

"Supper," she said. "I said you'd have them back at supper."

---

Quenna's note was under his door when he went up, folded once, in her square hand. *My room. Now. Not long.*

He went up the narrow stair at the back of the main hall and knocked, and she said *come*, and he went in past the cold grate to the desk under the window. She had a lamp lit and the shutters closed on the dark yard, and a single sheet of paper on the desk in front of her, and she did not tell him to sit, so he stood.

"Procedure," she said. "Then you can go to bed. Tomorrow you are the respondent answering a challenge under the charter, and the charter has a rule about that, and I want you to have heard it from me before you hear it from the magistrate." She put one finger on the sheet. "The respondent's primary presentation is made standing, and it is made uninterrupted. Nobody may break into it. Not the officer of record, not counsel, not a member of the panel. The magistrate may stop you for order and for nothing else. You may speak until you have finished, and you will be the one who decides when you have finished." She looked up. "That's very rare. Most people who stand on a floor like that never get it, because they don't speak for themselves. You do. So it's yours, all of it, from the first word to the last. I'd like you to know it's there before you need it."

"Uninterrupted."

"Uninterrupted. If the Warden stands up in the middle, the magistrate will sit him down. He knows that. He'll stand up anyway, if he thinks it's worth being sat down." She turned the sheet over, and there was nothing on the back of it. "That's the procedure. Now the other thing."

She was quiet for a while, and he waited, because he had learned in twenty weeks that a Quenna who was quiet was a Quenna choosing which of several true things to say.

"Today I sat beside you," she said. "I'll sit beside you tomorrow, if you want me there. I'm your assessor of record. Nobody will think anything of it. It's where I'm supposed to be."

He had known she would offer. He had thought about it, on and off, since the stair after the council in the nineteenth week, and he had decided, and the deciding had not been easy, and it was not easy now that she was in front of him.

"I'd like you not to," he said.

Quenna did not move.

"It isn't that I don't want you there." He made himself go on, because she had earned the whole of it and not a piece. "It's that tomorrow has to be mine. Every word of it. If you're sitting beside me, then whatever I say, half the room will think it's the academy's argument with a boy standing up to say it. Your drafting, with my name on the front. And the other half will think you're there to catch me if I fall." He looked at the blank side of the sheet on her desk. "Nobody's ever let me say what I am. Not at the station, not in Ardenmere, not on the form. It was always somebody else's words, written over my head. I've spent three weeks earning the right to stand up and say this one thing myself. I'd like the room to see me do it by myself."

For a long time Quenna said nothing, and the lamp hissed between them.

He watched her take it, and watched what it cost her, and did not look away from any of it. She had staked her name on him in front of a panel when nobody else would. She had stood in a chalk square that afternoon and chosen the truth over him, and then come back and sat down beside him as if nothing had happened. Now he was asking her to stand up from that chair in front of the whole room and move it, so that everybody could see her not protect him.

"A row back," she said at last. "Behind the table. Close enough that you'll know where I am. Far enough that the record will show a respondent who answered for himself." She pulled the sheet toward her and folded it in half, and then in half again. "That's the last thing I can give you on that floor, you understand. Taking the chair away."

"I understand."

"I don't think you do. Not yet." Quenna stood up, which meant he should go. "You will in about twenty years, when somebody asks it of you." She went to the door and held it for him, and on the threshold she stopped him with two fingers on his sleeve, very lightly, which she had never done before. "Go to sleep. You never do the night before. Tonight you will, because I'm telling you to, and because there's nothing left to do."

He did sleep.

It did not come at once. He lay in the dark of the narrow room with his coat on the chair and the two papers in its pocket, and the fear came and lay down beside him as it always did on the night before a floor, the same size it had always been. He let it. Under it there was the feeling he had found on the eve and had not lost: something in the middle of him that did not move. He thought of Naveth taking his spectacles off in the chalk square, and of Quenna drawing the same short line on her slate over and over. He thought of a girl on the front bench who had stopped writing, and of Hobb, and of a man packing his cases in order, who had looked up once.

Before he put out the lamp he had written one line in the binder, in the square hand, under the eve's.

*The first sitting is his. Every word true. Tomorrow I stand up alone, and nobody may interrupt me.*

He read it once and did not change it, and the last thing he heard before he slept was the town's lamps going out below the wall, one by one, though he could not have heard that, and knew he could not, and heard it anyway.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-03-no-path-given/manuscript/chapter-57.md

# Chapter 57 — Shall Mean

Prynn brought the code herself.

Cael saw her come in at the side door of the assembly room a little before the first bell, while the benches were still filling, and for a moment he did not understand what he was looking at. She was carrying the first volume of the charter against her chest in both arms, as she had carried it to the long table on the first full morning of the three weeks, as if it might break. Behind her came Hobb, with a stack of dark-spined volumes balanced on his forearms from his elbows to his chin, and the bundled fascicles with their faded tapes hanging over the top of the stack like the roots of something dug up whole. Behind Hobb came Brom with the rest.

They set it all down on the respondent's table, the charter's three volumes and the schedule's seven and the fascicles in their order, and Prynn squared each one against the edge as it went down, with the side of her hand, until the bare table of yesterday had a low dark wall along its far side. Then she stood back and looked at it.

"Mine," she said. "Not theirs. If you're going to read the code to a magistrate you'll read it off a copy that's been kept properly." She looked at him over her spectacles. "I've counted them. Every fascicle. Don't drop anything."

"I won't."

"See you don't." She turned to Hobb. "Thank you. You can go and sit down." Hobb went. She turned to Brom. "You too. The front bench. I've kept it."

She had. Cael looked round at the gallery and saw that the whole middle of the front bench was empty, five places of it, in a room where people were already standing in the passage, and that nobody had dared to sit there. Prynn went and sat down at the end of the empty places with her arms folded, and Brom sat down beside her, and a moment later Karis came along the bench from the aisle and sat beside Brom, and Lira beside Karis. That left one place, between Lira and the next person along, an old woman from the town who had plainly been warned. On the empty place Prynn had laid a slip of paper, folded once, of the narrow kind she put in a gap on a shelf where a volume had been taken down.

Nobody asked her what it was for. Cael did not ask either. He looked at it a moment, and then away.

The room was fuller than yesterday, if that was possible. The people who did not belong to the hill had multiplied overnight. There were two more grey coats, and a man who had the look of an academy's provost about him and sat beside the woman in dark blue, and a cluster of people from the town at the back who could not have said a word about the charter and had come anyway, because somewhere over the winter the town had decided that the observer was its own. He saw the draper, who let a room over her shop to people's mothers, standing by the back wall with her shawl over her head. He saw the station clerk from the grey building at the corner, without his ledger. He did not see the old woman in black with the cushion, and then he did; she had moved nearer the front.

Oona was on the front bench at the left by the aisle, in the same place as yesterday, with her slate on her knees. She was not writing. She was looking at the respondent's table with her whole face.

Quenna came in by the side door and did not come to the table. She went to the row of chairs behind it, set a little back from it, where the academy's counsel and Naveth already sat, and took the chair at the end nearest the table, and sat down in it, and put her slate on her knee. She did not look at him. Cael felt her there exactly as she had said he would.

He sat down alone at the respondent's table, in front of the low dark wall of books, and put his hands flat on the wood for a moment, and took them off again.

---

The panel came in at the bell, and the room stood and sat.

Yorlan had the thin book under his arm. The grey-bearded assessor had his pad. And Ilsev, coming last, carried in her two arms a set of dark-spined volumes of her own, tied with a strap, which she had not brought the day before. She set them down on the bench in front of her chair and unbuckled the strap and laid them out in a row, and opened the first of them at a place near its beginning, and put one finger on the page.

Cael saw Coss see it. He saw the Warden's eyes go to the row of volumes in front of the senior evaluation seat, and stay there for a breath, and come away.

Yorlan opened his book.

"This tribunal sits under the charter, in public, and on the record. The matter is named in the notice. The parties are named in the notice. The panel will hear the challenge first, and the answer after. The panel will hear what is put to it and no more. The panel will not hear argument for which it has not been shown the ground. The respondent will state his name."

It was the same. Every word was the same as yesterday, at the same pace, in the same order, and somewhere behind him in the gallery Cael heard a small sound that might have been Karis, and might have been a laugh.

He stood. "Caelen Hesk-ward."

"The panel notes it." Yorlan did not tell him to sit. He turned a page in the thin book, and read from it in the same voice. "At the close of yesterday's sitting the respondent put a question to this panel about the reach of its own hand. A tribunal may always be asked how far its hand goes. It may never decline to look." He turned another page. "The panel has looked. The question is before it. The respondent will be heard standing, and while he is heard nobody will break in on him, from either table or from this bench, except the panel for order." He looked up. "The floor is the respondent's."

He sat back in the high-backed chair.

Cael stood alone in the middle of two hundred people who had stopped breathing.

He knew this quiet. It was the quiet of the main floor at the Ironyard in the instant before Vell lifted her hand for the first exchange, when the rails have stopped shouting their odds and six hundred people are only waiting. It was the formal yard with the frost still white in the cracks of the flags and Karis across the chalk, laying her first point. He had never once stood in it with less to lose in his body and more to lose in his life. He laid both palms on the table for the space of a heartbeat, as he had laid them on the edge of every ring since Ardenmere, and felt the grain of the wood, and through it, very faintly where he leaned, the press of a folded page and a pencilled slip against his chest.

*This is real. Start from that.*

He took his hands off the wood and began.

"The Warden is right," he said.

He let the four words go out into the room by themselves and stand there, because they had cost him three weeks and they were worth a breath of quiet.

"Everything he showed you yesterday is so. The provision is a kindness written for children who haven't yet been to a station, and his drafting records show it, signed, and when the committee was asked whether it should reach somebody like me it said no and wrote down why. I've got nothing to say against any of that, and I won't waste the panel's patience pretending I have. I went to a station. I went in Denvash, at fourteen, on a day the registry holds. So the form describes somebody I'm not, and an enrollment that stands on that form doesn't stand. I give the Warden all of it. Every part."

Nothing came from the gallery at all.

He kept his eyes on the bench, because he had promised himself on the stair that he would look nowhere else. But he could feel the room behind him, and he felt it change, the way a crowd round a ring changes when the fighter it came to cheer lowers his hands. Half the benches, he knew without turning, had just decided he had come in to surrender.

Yorlan leaned a very little way forward, and did the next thing the procedure gave him to do. Afterward Cael thought that this, more than anything he had said himself, was what made it real.
